Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I really appreciate it, and I hope I'm not taking up time that someone else might need more. 

I suppose what brings me in is... well, it's a little embarrassing, if I'm being honest. It's probably just a silly habit, really. But lately, once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it just gets a bit out of hand. Well, I suppose it depends on what you'd call often. It's really not every night, nothing extreme like that. Looking back on it... I guess it’s been happening at least once most weeks. Well, I... I wouldn't use the word "completely," no. That sounds so dramatic, doesn't it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm just ravenous or totally out of my own mind. 

But, if I'm being perfectly honest with you—and I do want to be honest, I know that's why I'm here—it's just that... well, once I start picking at those leftovers, it does feel a little bit like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, "That's enough now, Alejandro." But... my hand just sort of keeps going back for another little bite anyway. 

It's not a complete loss of control, I promise. It's just a bit harder to stop than I'd like to admit. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the clearest picture I can.

It's probably just a silly habit, really. But lately, once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it just gets a bit out of hand. It feels like the sensible part of me is right there in my head telling me that is enough, Alejandro, but... my hand just keeps going back for more anyway. \n\nIt's not a terrible thing, I know, but I just feel a bit foolish that I can't seem to stop myself in those moments. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it the way you need me to.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of picking at leftovers late at night happen often, and do they ever make you feel upset, guilty, or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it depends on what you'd call often. It's really not every night, nothing extreme like that. Looking back on it... I guess it’s been happening at least once most weeks. Maybe two nights a week sometimes? It really only started being a bit of a routine since my cousin moved in with me. We just have bigger dinners and more food around now, you see. \n\nAnd about feeling upset... well, maybe just a little bit. I do feel a bit foolish the next morning, maybe a tiny bit embarrassed that a man my age can't just close the fridge and go to bed. But I try not to make a big fuss over it. I mean, there are much worse problems a person could have, right? \n\nI hope that answers your question okay, Doctor. Am I giving you the right kind of information?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, does it feel like you’re not fully in control of your actions, like you can’t stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that's a fair way to put it. It's... it's exactly like I was mentioning earlier. I'll be standing there, and the sensible part of my brain is right there, clear as day, saying, \"Alright, Alejandro, that's quite enough for one evening.\" But... well, it's almost like my hand just isn't listening to my head. It just keeps reaching out for another bite, even when I know I should stop. \n\nI don't want to make it sound like I'm completely out of my mind or anything dramatic like that. It's just a silly lack of willpower in the moment, I'm sure. But yes, it does feel a bit like I can't quite rein myself in once I start. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about, Doctor? I hope I'm making sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this behavior ever caused you significant distress, or interfered with your daily life, such as affecting your relationships, work, or health?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing severe like that. I want to make sure you know I'm still keeping up with all my normal day-to-day things—my errands, my appointments, helping out around the house... I certainly wouldn't let a silly habit get in the way of my responsibilities. \n\nBut... well, I suppose the mornings after it happens, I do feel a bit... sluggish? Just a little slow to get going. And, if I'm being perfectly honest, I feel a little less proud of how I'm handling myself. Just a bit disappointed, I guess. But I always push right through it and get on with my day! \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just don't want you to think I'm completely falling apart over a few leftovers, Doctor. Am I doing okay explaining all of this?\n\nDoctor: Before you start picking at the leftovers, do you ever feel a sense of tension or a strong urge that builds up before the behavior begins?\n\nPatient: Well, tension... I'm not really sure I'd call it that. It's not like I'm pacing the floor or pulling my hair out before I go to the kitchen. Nothing dramatic. \n\nI suppose sometimes I just... think about the food being there. You know, you're sitting up reading or watching television, and you just remember that there's still some of that nice roast chicken in the fridge. It's more of a passing thought than a strong urge, really. Just a little notion that maybe a quick bite would be nice before bed. \n\nI don't think I feel any kind of big build-up or anything like that. Just a normal, everyday sort of feeling. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm trying to be as accurate as I can for you.\n\nDoctor: After you've had a bite or two—or more—of the leftovers, does it give you a sense of relief, satisfaction, or pleasure in that moment?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, maybe a little bit of satisfaction right at first. It tastes nice, of course, and it's a pleasant little treat. \n\nBut I wouldn't say it's some sort of grand relief or anything like that. It's really just the simple pleasure of a good piece of chicken or a nice bite of potatoes. Anyone would enjoy that, wouldn't they? \n\nI just... I don't want to make it sound like I'm eating for some sort of emotional thrill. It's just a quick snack that tastes good in the moment. I hope that's an alright answer, Doctor? I'm not trying to be difficult, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if this behavior has ever led to any problems with your mood, your relationships, or your ability to function at work or home, beyond just feeling a little sluggish or disappointed the next morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, absolutely not. I promise you, it hasn't caused any real problems like that. \n\nMy wife and I get along just wonderfully, and I still take care of the yard work and pay the bills on time. I would never let a silly little habit like having a late-night snack interfere with my marriage or my responsibilities around the house. I'm retired, you know, so I don't have a boss to answer to anymore, but if I did, I'm sure I'd be right there on time every morning! \n\nIt's really just that little bit of sluggishness I mentioned before, and maybe feeling a bit foolish about it all. But nothing that ruins my day or causes any trouble for anyone else. I wouldn't let it get to that point. \n\nDoes that help clear things up, Doctor? I just want to reassure you that I'm keeping everything together just fine.\n\nDoctor: Before you start picking at the leftovers, do you ever feel a particular urge, discomfort, or sensation that feels like it needs to be acted on?\n\nPatient: Well, I know we touched on something a bit like this just a few minutes ago, but I certainly don't mind answering again if it helps. I... no, I really wouldn't say there's any discomfort or a feeling that I absolutely have to act on. \n\nIt's not like an itch I just have to scratch, if you see what I mean. I promise I'm not trying to hide anything from you, it's honestly just that quiet little thought that a snack sounds nice. There's no physical sensation or heavy urge pushing me toward the kitchen, nothing intense like that at all. \n\nAm I making sense, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm explaining myself properly so I'm giving you the right information and not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the moment of picking at the leftovers, does it ever feel like you've completely lost control, as if you can't stop eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't use the word \"completely,\" no. That sounds so dramatic, doesn't it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm just ravenous or totally out of my own mind. \n\nBut, if I'm being perfectly honest with you—and I do want to be honest, I know that's why I'm here—it's just that... well, once I start picking at those leftovers, it does feel a little bit like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, \"That's enough now, Alejandro.\" But... my hand just sort of keeps going back for another little bite anyway. \n\nIt's not a complete loss of control, I promise. It's just a bit harder to stop than I'd like to admit. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the clearest picture I can.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: As you're actually picking at the leftovers and eating them, do you feel a sense of comfort, pleasure, or relief while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. It's not... I really don't think it's about comfort or feeling any kind of relief. I certainly hope you don't think I'm sitting in the dark having a grand old time, or using food to soothe some sort of emotional problem! \n\nIt's really just... well, just chewing on a bit of cold chicken or whatever happens to be in the container. There isn't any big wave of pleasure, I promise you. I just sort of do it without getting any real joy out of it at all. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right details so I can be as helpful as possible.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating the leftovers, do you ever feel like you can't stop eating even if you want to, like the eating takes over for a while?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, did I not explain that well enough a moment ago? I'm so sorry if I was being confusing, Doctor. I know we just touched on this, and I want to make sure I'm being helpful. \n\nLike I mentioned before, it's not like some... some outside force takes over my body or anything dramatic like that. I'd hate for you to think I'm completely out of my wits! It's really just that late at night, once I start picking at those leftovers, the sensible part of my brain is right there saying, 'That's enough now, Alejandro.' But... well, my hand just keeps going back for more anyway, even when a part of me wants to stop. \n\nI wouldn't say the eating completely takes over, it's just... a bit harder to walk away from than it should be. It's just a bit harder to stop than I'd like to admit. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the clearest picture I can.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: As you're actually picking at the leftovers and eating them, do you feel a sense of comfort, pleasure, or relief while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. It's not... I really don't think it's about comfort or feeling any kind of relief. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh my, I feel like I'm doing a terrible job of explaining this to you, Doctor. I'm so sorry, I really don't mean to make you ask the same thing over and over. I must be frustrating you. 

I don't know if I'd use the words 'couldn't resist the urge'—that makes it sound like I'm battling some sort of monster in the kitchen! I'd hate for you to think I'm completely out of control. But... well, just like I was trying to say a moment ago, it's that late at night, I'll be standing there, and my sensible side is very clearly telling me, 'Alejandro, put the lid back on, that is quite enough.' And I do want to listen to that voice. I really do try to just walk away. But... my hand just sort of betrays me and goes back for another bite anyway. 

I suppose I just don't stop it, even though a big part of me is trying to. Does that count as what you're asking? I really hope I'm finally giving you the information you need.

I hope I'm explaining it the way you need me to.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of picking at leftovers late at night happen often, and do they ever make you feel upset, guilty, or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it depends on what you'd call often. It's really not every night, nothing extreme like that. Looking back on it... I guess it’s been happening at least once most weeks. Maybe two nights a week sometimes? It really only started being a bit of a routine since my cousin moved in with me. We just have bigger dinners and more food around now, you see. \n\nAnd about feeling upset... well, maybe just a little bit. I do feel a bit foolish the next morning, maybe a tiny bit embarrassed that a man my age can't just close the fridge and go to bed. But I try not to make a big fuss over it. I mean, there are much worse problems a person could have, right? \n\nI hope that answers your question okay, Doctor. Am I giving you the right kind of information?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, does it feel like you’re not fully in control of your actions, like you can’t stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that's a fair way to put it. It's... it's exactly like I was mentioning earlier. I'll be standing there, and the sensible part of my brain is right there, clear as day, saying, \"Alright, Alejandro, that's quite enough for one evening.\" But... well, it's almost like my hand just isn't listening to my head. It just keeps reaching out for another bite, even when I know I should stop. \n\nI don't want to make it sound like I'm completely out of my mind or anything dramatic like that. It's just a silly lack of willpower in the moment, I'm sure. But yes, it does feel a bit like I can't quite rein myself in once I start. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about, Doctor? I hope I'm making sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this behavior ever caused you significant distress, or interfered with your daily life, such as affecting your relationships, work, or health?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing severe like that. I certainly wouldn't let a silly habit get in the way of my responsibilities. \n\nBut... well, I suppose the mornings after it happens, I do feel a bit... sluggish? Just a little slow to get going. And, if I'm being perfectly honest, I feel a little less proud of how I'm handling myself. Just a bit disappointed, I guess. But I always push right through it and get on with my day! \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just don't want you to think I'm completely falling apart over a few leftovers, Doctor. Nothing dramatic. \n\nI suppose sometimes I just... think about the food being there. I hope I'm making sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this behavior ever caused you significant distress, or interfered with your daily life, such as affecting your relationships, work, or health?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing severe like that. I want to make sure you know I'm still keeping up with all my normal day-to-day things—my errands, my appointments, helping out around the house... I certainly wouldn't let a silly habit get in the way of my responsibilities. \n\nBut... well, I suppose the mornings after it happens, I do feel a bit... sluggish? Just a little slow to get going. And, if I'm being perfectly honest, I feel a little less proud of how I'm handling myself. Just a bit disappointed, I guess. But I always push right through it and get on with my day! \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just don't want you to think I'm completely falling apart over a few leftovers, Doctor. Am I doing okay explaining all of this?\n\nDoctor: Before you start picking at the leftovers, do you ever feel a sense of tension or a strong urge that builds up before the behavior begins?\n\nPatient: Well, tension... I'm not really sure I'd call it that. It's not like I'm pacing the floor or pulling my hair out before I go to the kitchen. Nothing dramatic. \n\nI suppose sometimes I just... think about the food being there. You know, you're sitting up reading or watching television, and you just remember that there's still some of that nice roast chicken in the fridge. It's more of a passing thought than a strong urge, really. Just a little notion that maybe a quick bite would be nice before bed. \n\nI don't think I feel any kind of big build-up or anything like that. Just a normal, everyday sort of feeling. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm trying to be as accurate as I can for you.\n\nDoctor: After you've had a bite or two—or more—of the leftovers, does it give you a sense of relief, satisfaction, or pleasure in that moment?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, maybe a little bit of satisfaction right at first. It tastes nice, of course, and it's a pleasant little treat. \n\nBut I wouldn't say it's some sort of grand relief or anything like that. It's really just the simple pleasure of a good piece of chicken or a nice bite of potatoes. Anyone would enjoy that, wouldn't they? \n\nI just... I don't want to make it sound like I'm eating for some sort of emotional thrill. It's just a quick snack that tastes good in the moment. I hope that's an alright answer, Doctor? I'm not trying to be difficult, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if this behavior has ever led to any problems with your mood, your relationships, or your ability to function at work or home, beyond just feeling a little sluggish or disappointed the next morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, absolutely not. I promise you, it hasn't caused any real problems like that. \n\nMy wife and I get along just wonderfully, and I still take care of the yard work and pay the bills on time. I would never let a silly little habit like having a late-night snack interfere with my marriage or my responsibilities around the house. I'm retired, you know, so I don't have a boss to answer to anymore, but if I did, I'm sure I'd be right there on time every morning! \n\nIt's really just that little bit of sluggishness I mentioned before, and maybe feeling a bit foolish about it all. But nothing that ruins my day or causes any trouble for anyone else. I wouldn't let it get to that point. \n\nDoes that help clear things up, Doctor? I just want to reassure you that I'm keeping everything together just fine.\n\nDoctor: Before you start picking at the leftovers, do you ever feel a particular urge, discomfort, or sensation that feels like it needs to be acted on?\n\nPatient: Well, I know we touched on something a bit like this just a few minutes ago, but I certainly don't mind answering again if it helps. I... no, I really wouldn't say there's any discomfort or a feeling that I absolutely have to act on. \n\nIt's not like an itch I just have to scratch, if you see what I mean. I promise I'm not trying to hide anything from you, it's honestly just that quiet little thought that a snack sounds nice. There's no physical sensation or heavy urge pushing me toward the kitchen, nothing intense like that at all. \n\nAm I making sense, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm explaining myself properly so I'm giving you the right information and not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the moment of picking at the leftovers, does it ever feel like you've completely lost control, as if you can't stop eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't use the word \"completely,\" no. That sounds so dramatic, doesn't it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm just ravenous or totally out of my own mind. \n\nBut, if I'm being perfectly honest with you—and I do want to be honest, I know that's why I'm here—it's just that... well, once I start picking at those leftovers, it does feel a little bit like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, \"That's enough now, Alejandro.\" But... my hand just sort of keeps going back for another little bite anyway. \n\nIt's not a complete loss of control, I promise. It's just a bit harder to stop than I'd like to admit. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the clearest picture I can.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: As you're actually picking at the leftovers and eating them, do you feel a sense of comfort, pleasure, or relief while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. It's not... I really don't think it's about comfort or feeling any kind of relief. I certainly hope you don't think I'm sitting in the dark having a grand old time, or using food to soothe some sort of emotional problem! \n\nIt's really just... well, just chewing on a bit of cold chicken or whatever happens to be in the container. There isn't any big wave of pleasure, I promise you. I just sort of do it without getting any real joy out of it at all. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right details so I can be as helpful as possible.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating the leftovers, do you ever feel like you can't stop eating even if you want to, like the eating takes over for a while?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, did I not explain that well enough a moment ago? I'm so sorry if I was being confusing, Doctor. I know we just touched on this, and I want to make sure I'm being helpful. \n\nLike I mentioned before, it's not like some... some outside force takes over my body or anything dramatic like that. I'd hate for you to think I'm completely out of my wits! It's really just that late at night, once I start picking at those leftovers, the sensible part of my brain is right there saying, 'That's enough now, Alejandro.' But... well, my hand just keeps going back for more anyway, even when a part of me wants to stop. \n\nI wouldn't say the eating completely takes over, it's just... a bit harder to walk away from than it should be. Does that clarify things a bit better? I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers and not making you repeat yourself on my account.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you tried very hard to stop yourself from picking at the leftovers, but no matter how hard you tried, you just couldn't resist the urge?\n\nPatient: Oh my, I feel like I'm doing a terrible job of explaining this to you, Doctor. But... my hand just sort of betrays me and goes back for another bite anyway. \n\nI suppose I just don't stop it, even though a big part of me is trying to. Does that count as what you're asking? I don't want to make it sound like I'm eating for some sort of emotional thrill. It's just a quick snack that tastes good in the moment. I hope that's an alright answer, Doctor? I'm not trying to be difficult, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if this behavior has ever led to any problems with your mood, your relationships, or your ability to function at work or home, beyond just feeling a little sluggish or disappointed the next morning?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, absolutely not. I promise you, it hasn't caused any real problems like that. \n\nMy wife and I get along just wonderfully, and I still take care of the yard work and pay the bills on time. I would never let a silly little habit like having a late-night snack interfere with my marriage or my responsibilities around the house. I'm retired, you know, so I don't have a boss to answer to anymore, but if I did, I'm sure I'd be right there on time every morning! \n\nIt's really just that little bit of sluggishness I mentioned before, and maybe feeling a bit foolish about it all. But nothing that ruins my day or causes any trouble for anyone else. I wouldn't let it get to that point. \n\nDoes that help clear things up, Doctor? I... no, I really wouldn't say there's any discomfort or a feeling that I absolutely have to act on. \n\nIt's not like an itch I just have to scratch, if you see what I mean. It's just a bit harder to stop than I'd like to admit. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the clearest picture I can.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: As you're actually picking at the leftovers and eating them, do you feel a sense of comfort, pleasure, or relief while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. Oh, well, I... I suppose I do eat more than I intend to. I'm so sorry to admit that, it sounds terribly gluttonous, doesn't it? Please don't think too badly of me. 

It's just that... well, some nights after supper, when I'm supposed to be putting away the leftovers—like the tamales or the mole—I'll end up eating... well, a bit more than I should. Sometimes several servings. And then maybe some pan dulce out in the garage. 

It's certainly more than I'd ever put on a plate if my family was watching me. I know it's not right, and I feel quite foolish even saying it out loud to you. I wouldn't say I'm eating at lightning speed or anything like that, but the amount... yes, it's larger than I mean to eat. 

Is that the kind of information you need, Doctor? I hope I'm not shocking you too much. I just want to be honest so you can help me.

I promise you, it hasn't caused any real problems like that. \n\nMy wife and I get along just wonderfully, and I still take care of the yard work and pay the bills on time. I would never let a silly little habit like having a late-night snack interfere with my marriage or my responsibilities around the house. I'm retired, you know, so I don't have a boss to answer to anymore, but if I did, I'm sure I'd be right there on time every morning! \n\nIt's really just that little bit of sluggishness I mentioned before, and maybe feeling a bit foolish about it all. But nothing that ruins my day or causes any trouble for anyone else. I wouldn't let it get to that point. \n\nDoes that help clear things up, Doctor? I just want to reassure you that I'm keeping everything together just fine.\n\nDoctor: Before you start picking at the leftovers, do you ever feel a particular urge, discomfort, or sensation that feels like it needs to be acted on?\n\nPatient: Well, I know we touched on something a bit like this just a few minutes ago, but I certainly don't mind answering again if it helps. I... no, I really wouldn't say there's any discomfort or a feeling that I absolutely have to act on. \n\nIt's not like an itch I just have to scratch, if you see what I mean. I promise I'm not trying to hide anything from you, it's honestly just that quiet little thought that a snack sounds nice. There's no physical sensation or heavy urge pushing me toward the kitchen, nothing intense like that at all. \n\nAm I making sense, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm explaining myself properly so I'm giving you the right information and not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the moment of picking at the leftovers, does it ever feel like you've completely lost control, as if you can't stop eating no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't use the word \"completely,\" no. That sounds so dramatic, doesn't it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm just ravenous or totally out of my own mind. \n\nBut, if I'm being perfectly honest with you—and I do want to be honest, I know that's why I'm here—it's just that... well, once I start picking at those leftovers, it does feel a little bit like the sensible part of me is sitting there saying, \"That's enough now, Alejandro.\" But... my hand just sort of keeps going back for another little bite anyway. \n\nIt's not a complete loss of control, I promise. It's just a bit harder to stop than I'd like to admit. I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the clearest picture I can.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: As you're actually picking at the leftovers and eating them, do you feel a sense of comfort, pleasure, or relief while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. It's not... I really don't think it's about comfort or feeling any kind of relief. I certainly hope you don't think I'm sitting in the dark having a grand old time, or using food to soothe some sort of emotional problem! \n\nIt's really just... well, just chewing on a bit of cold chicken or whatever happens to be in the container. There isn't any big wave of pleasure, I promise you. I just sort of do it without getting any real joy out of it at all. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right details so I can be as helpful as possible.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating the leftovers, do you ever feel like you can't stop eating even if you want to, like the eating takes over for a while?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, did I not explain that well enough a moment ago? I'm so sorry if I was being confusing, Doctor. I know we just touched on this, and I want to make sure I'm being helpful. \n\nLike I mentioned before, it's not like some... some outside force takes over my body or anything dramatic like that. I'd hate for you to think I'm completely out of my wits! It's really just that late at night, once I start picking at those leftovers, the sensible part of my brain is right there saying, 'That's enough now, Alejandro.' But... well, my hand just keeps going back for more anyway, even when a part of me wants to stop. \n\nI wouldn't say the eating completely takes over, it's just... a bit harder to walk away from than it should be. Does that clarify things a bit better? I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers and not making you repeat yourself on my account.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you tried very hard to stop yourself from picking at the leftovers, but no matter how hard you tried, you just couldn't resist the urge?\n\nPatient: Oh my, I feel like I'm doing a terrible job of explaining this to you, Doctor. I'm so sorry, I really don't mean to make you ask the same thing over and over. I must be frustrating you. \n\nI don't know if I'd use the words 'couldn't resist the urge'—that makes it sound like I'm battling some sort of monster in the kitchen! I'd hate for you to think I'm completely out of control. But... well, just like I was trying to say a moment ago, it's that late at night, I'll be standing there, and my sensible side is very clearly telling me, 'Alejandro, put the lid back on, that is quite enough.' And I do want to listen to that voice. I really do try to just walk away. But... my hand just sort of betrays me and goes back for another bite anyway. \n\nI suppose I just don't stop it, even though a big part of me is trying to. Does that count as what you're asking? I really hope I'm finally giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: When you're picking at the leftovers, do you feel like you're not fully in control of your actions, like you can't stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, I've really made a mess of this, haven't I? I am so, so sorry, Doctor. You're having to ask me the very same thing again, which means I must be doing a dreadful job of giving you a clear answer. Please forgive me, I don't mean to be difficult.\n\nI suppose... well, if you mean 'not fully in control' in the sense that I want to stop, but I just don't... then yes, I suppose that's accurate. It's exactly like I was trying to explain just a moment ago. The rational part of me is standing right there in the kitchen saying, 'Alejandro, that is quite enough, put the food away.' But my hand just seems to ignore that sensible voice and reaches for another bite anyway. \n\nI'm not totally out of my mind or anything, I just... I can't seem to make myself walk away when I know I should. Does that finally make sense? Please tell me I'm answering it properly this time. I'd hate to keep wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe exactly what you do when you pick at the leftovers? Do you find yourself repeating the same motions over and over, like picking at the food in a certain way or for a specific reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't... I don't think I do anything quite like that. I'm sorry, I hope that's not what you were looking for. \n\nI don't have any sort of... special routine or anything repetitive like that. It's really just me, standing by the counter, using a fork to take a bite here and there from whatever is left in the containers. I don't pick at it in any particular way or arrange it or anything peculiar. It's just simple eating, really. Just a bit too much of it, I suppose. \n\nIs that what you meant? I just eat it normally, I promise I'm not doing anything strange with the food. I hope that's an alright answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you ever feel anxious or physically uncomfortable before you start eating the leftovers, as if something bad might happen if you don’t eat them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I don't feel anything like that at all. I hope that's the right answer, I'd hate to disappoint you, but no... I don't feel anxious or worried that something terrible will happen. \n\nIt's really nothing so dramatic, Doctor. I promise I'm not feeling any sort of strange dread or anything. It's just... well, just me wanting a bit more of the food, even though I know I shouldn't. I don't feel uncomfortable or scared before I do it. I just sort of... wander into the kitchen. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making things difficult by not having those feelings you're asking about. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of what happens.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating the leftovers, do you feel like you're eating much more rapidly than normal, or in larger amounts than you intended, even if you weren't especially hungry?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, I... Sometimes several servings. And then maybe some pan dulce out in the garage. \n\nIt's certainly more than I'd ever put on a plate if my family was watching me. I know it's not right, and I feel quite foolish even saying it out loud to you. I do feel a bit foolish the next morning, maybe a tiny bit embarrassed that a man my age can't just close the fridge and go to bed. But I try not to make a big fuss over it. I mean, there are much worse problems a person could have, right? \n\nI hope that answers your question okay, Doctor. Am I giving you the right kind of information?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, does it feel like you’re not fully in control of your actions, like you can’t stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose that's a fair way to put it. It's... it's exactly like I was mentioning earlier. I'll be standing there, and the sensible part of my brain is right there, clear as day, saying, \"Alright, Alejandro, that's quite enough for one evening.\" But... well, it's almost like my hand just isn't listening to my head. It just keeps reaching out for another bite, even when I know I should stop. \n\nI don't want to make it sound like I'm completely out of my mind or anything dramatic like that. It's just a silly lack of willpower in the moment, I'm sure. But yes, it does feel a bit like I can't quite rein myself in once I start. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about, Doctor? I hope I'm making sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this behavior ever caused you significant distress, or interfered with your daily life, such as affecting your relationships, work, or health?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, nothing severe like that. I want to make sure you know I'm still keeping up with all my normal day-to-day things—my errands, my appointments, helping out around the house... I certainly wouldn't let a silly habit get in the way of my responsibilities. \n\nBut... well, I suppose the mornings after it happens, I do feel a bit... sluggish? Just a little slow to get going. And, if I'm being perfectly honest, I feel a little less proud of how I'm handling myself. Just a bit disappointed, I guess. But I always push right through it and get on with my day! \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just don't want you to think I'm completely falling apart over a few leftovers, Doctor. I'm not really sure I'd call it that. It's not like I'm pacing the floor or pulling my hair out before I go to the kitchen. It's more of a passing thought than a strong urge, really. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm trying to be as accurate as I can for you.\n\nDoctor: After you've had a bite or two—or more—of the leftovers, does it give you a sense of relief, satisfaction, or pleasure in that moment?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, maybe a little bit of satisfaction right at first. It tastes nice, of course, and it's a pleasant little treat. \n\nBut I wouldn't say it's some sort of grand relief or anything like that. It's really just the simple pleasure of a good piece of chicken or a nice bite of potatoes. Anyone would enjoy that, wouldn't they? \n\nI just... I don't want to make it sound like I'm eating for some sort of emotional thrill. It's just a quick snack that tastes good in the moment. I hope that's an alright answer, Doctor?
